<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 2 Focus 48 (Caudate Nucleus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structure most likely affected by a lesion causing specific motor and cognitive deficits, given its location within the basal ganglia and its connections. (鉴于其在基底神经节内的位置及其连接，识别受损导致特定运动和认知缺陷的解剖结构。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided hemiparesis and dysarthria. He also reports difficulty initiating movements and exhibits cognitive slowing, particularly with tasks requiring executive function. Neurological examination reveals weakness predominantly affecting the right upper limb (proximal > distal) and lower limb, with increased tone and hyperreflexia. MRI of the brain shows a well-defined, non-enhancing lesion in the left hemisphere. The lesion is located deep within the cerebral white matter, anterior to the internal capsule and lateral to the anterior limb of the internal capsule, extending into the region of the anterior commissure. The lesion appears to involve the tail of the caudate nucleus and the adjacent anterior limb of the internal capsule. Which structure is most likely affected by this lesion, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Putamen
(B) Globus Pallidus
(C) Substantia Nigra
(D) Subthalamic Nucleus
(E) Caudate Nucleus

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, sudden onset right hemiparesis, dysarthria, difficulty initiating movement (akinesia/bradykinesia), cognitive slowing (executive dysfunction). This constellation points towards a lesion affecting motor control and cognitive processes, likely involving the basal ganglia or related pathways.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis), increased tone (rigidity/spasticity), hyperreflexia. These are signs of upper motor neuron involvement, often seen with lesions affecting the corticospinal tract or structures influencing it, such as the basal ganglia. The pattern of weakness (proximal > distal) is less specific but can occur.
- **MRI Findings:** Left hemisphere lesion, deep white matter, anterior to the internal capsule, lateral to the anterior limb of the internal capsule, extending into the anterior commissure region. This precise location is crucial. The description points directly to the area occupied by the head and tail of the caudate nucleus and the adjacent anterior limb of the internal capsule.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is *most likely* affected, linking the clinical presentation and MRI findings.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Caudate Nucleus Anatomy:** The caudate nucleus is a C-shaped structure forming part of the basal ganglia. It has a head, body, and tail. The head is located deep within the cerebral hemispheres, lateral to the anterior limb of the internal capsule and anterior to the anterior commissure. The body lies lateral to the anterior two-thirds of the internal capsule. The tail (nucleus caudatus) curves around the thalamus and extends into the temporal lobe, anterior to the amygdala.
- **Location Correlation:** The MRI description ("anterior to the internal capsule and lateral to the anterior limb of the internal capsule, extending into the region of the anterior commissure") precisely matches the location of the head of the caudate nucleus. The involvement of the tail is also mentioned, which is consistent with the lesion's extent.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Lesions affecting the caudate nucleus, particularly the head and anterior limb of the internal capsule, are associated with motor deficits (hemiparesis, rigidity, bradykinesia) due to disruption of basal ganglia circuits influencing the motor cortex via the thalamus. Cognitive deficits, especially executive dysfunction, are also common because the caudate nucleus has extensive connections with the prefrontal cortex, crucial for executive functions. The patient's symptoms (hemiparesis, dysarthria, difficulty initiating movement, cognitive slowing) are classic manifestations of basal ganglia dysfunction, specifically involving the caudate nucleus and its connections.
- **Embryology:** The basal ganglia nuclei develop from the telencephalon. The caudate nucleus and putamen develop from the dorsal telencephalon (striatum), while the globus pallidus and substantia nigra develop from the ventral telencephalon. The subthalamic nucleus develops from the diencephalon. This embryological origin is less relevant to the specific anatomical localization question but provides context.
- **Blood Supply:** The caudate nucleus receives blood supply primarily from the anterior cerebral artery (ACA) and the middle cerebral artery (MCA), particularly the lenticulostriate branches. A stroke affecting these vessels could cause a lesion in this location.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Putamen:** The putamen is the lateral part of the striatum. It lies lateral to the globus pallidus and posterior to the caudate nucleus. While lesions here also cause basal ganglia symptoms, the described MRI location (anterior to the internal capsule, lateral to the anterior limb, near the anterior commissure) is characteristic of the caudate head, not the putamen. The putamen is more closely associated with the posterior limb of the internal capsule.
- (B) **Globus Pallidus:** The globus pallidus is medial to the putamen and lateral to the internal capsule. It consists of an internal (GPi) and external (GPe) segment. Lesions here cause similar motor symptoms but are located medially relative to the described lesion site. The GPi is a major output nucleus of the basal ganglia.
- (C) **Substantia Nigra:** The substantia nigra is located in the midbrain, ventral to the thalamus. It is a key structure in the basal ganglia circuit, particularly involved in Parkinson's disease (degeneration of dopaminergic neurons). A lesion here would not be located in the described region of the cerebral white matter.
- (D) **Subthalamic Nucleus:** The subthalamic nucleus is located ventral to the thalamus, medial to the internal capsule. Lesions here typically cause hemiballismus (violent, involuntary flinging movements), which is not described in the patient. Its location is also distinct from the described MRI findings.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure          | Location Relative to Internal Capsule | Location Relative to Anterior Commissure | Key Clinical Features (Lesion)                                  | MRI Location Match? |
|--------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------|
| **Caudate Nucleus** | Head: Lateral to Ant. Limb; Body: Lateral to Ant 2/3 Limb | Head: Anterior                               | Motor (bradykinesia, rigidity), Cognitive (executive dysfunction) | **Yes**             |
| Putamen            | Lateral to Globus Pallidus, Post. to Caudate | N/A                                      | Motor (bradykinesia, rigidity)                                   | No                  |
| Globus Pallidus    | Medial to Putamen, Lateral to Int. Capsule | N/A                                      | Motor (rigidity, dystonia)                                       | No                  |
| Substantia Nigra   | Midbrain                               | N/A                                      | Parkinsonism (tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia)                   | No                  |
| Subthalamic Nucleus| Ventral to Thalamus, Medial to Int. Capsule | N/A                                      | Hemiballismus                                                    | No                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The caudate nucleus, particularly its head, is located anterior to the anterior limb of the internal capsule and anterior to the anterior commissure. Lesions in this area disrupt basal ganglia circuits, leading to motor symptoms (like bradykinesia, rigidity) and cognitive deficits (especially executive dysfunction). Differentiating the caudate nucleus from the putamen, globus pallidus, and other basal ganglia structures relies on precise knowledge of their spatial relationships within the brain, especially relative to the internal capsule and other landmarks like the anterior commissure. Remember the caudate tail extends into the temporal lobe.

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 2 Focus 49 (nucleus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structure within the basal ganglia complex based on its location relative to other structures and its connections, specifically focusing on the subthalamic nucleus (STN) and its relationship to the internal capsule and thalamus. (识别基底神经节复合体内的解剖结构，基于其相对于其他结构的位置及其连接，特别是关注苍白球外侧部（STN）与内囊和丘脑的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of involuntary, violent, flinging movements of his limbs, particularly on the right side. His wife reports that these movements started abruptly while he was watching television and have been occurring intermittently for the past hour. The patient has a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia, managed with medication. Neurological examination reveals severe right-sided hemiballismus, characterized by large-amplitude, proximal limb flinging movements. His vital signs are stable. A non-contrast head CT scan is performed, which shows a small, acute hemorrhage in the region of the internal capsule, specifically contralateral to the side of the hemiballismus. Given the location of the hemorrhage and the resulting clinical presentation, which of the following structures is most likely affected?

(A) Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi)
(B) Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc)
(C) Subthalamic Nucleus (STN)
(D) Caudate Nucleus
(E) Putamen

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with sudden onset right-sided hemiballismus. Hemiballismus is a hyperkinetic movement disorder characterized by violent, involuntary flinging movements of the limbs, typically affecting the proximal muscles on one side of the body.
- **History:** Hypertension and hyperlipidemia are risk factors for stroke, including hemorrhagic stroke.
- **Neurological Examination:** Severe right-sided hemiballismus confirms a lesion affecting the motor control pathways, specifically on the left side (contralateral control).
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a small, acute hemorrhage in the left internal capsule. This is the critical piece of information linking the location of the lesion to the clinical signs.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely affected by the hemorrhage in the left internal capsule, causing right-sided hemiballismus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Anatomy:** The basal ganglia are a group of subcortical nuclei involved in motor control, learning, habit formation, and emotion. Key components include the striatum (caudate nucleus and putamen), globus pallidus (internal and external segments), subthalamic nucleus (STN), and substantia nigra (pars compacta and pars reticulata).
- **Location of STN:** The subthalamic nucleus (STN) is a small, lens-shaped nucleus located ventral to the thalamus, medial to the internal capsule, and superior to the substantia nigra. It is situated just lateral to the posterior commissure.
- **Internal Capsule:** The internal capsule is a white matter structure containing ascending and descending tracts, separating the thalamus and caudate nucleus medially from the lentiform nucleus (putamen and globus pallidus) laterally. It has anterior, genu, posterior, retrolenticular, and sublenticular parts.
- **STN and Internal Capsule Relationship:** The STN lies immediately medial to the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule. Therefore, a lesion (like a hemorrhage) in the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule can directly damage the STN.
- **STN Function and Hemiballismus:** The STN is a crucial component of the indirect pathway of the basal ganglia. It provides excitatory glutamatergic input to the globus pallidus internus (GPi) and substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr). Lesions of the STN (or its projections) lead to decreased inhibition of the thalamus by the GPi/SNr. This disinhibition results in excessive thalamocortical drive to the motor cortex, causing hyperkinetic movements like hemiballismus.
- **Other Structures:**
    - **GPi:** Located lateral to the internal capsule, within the lentiform nucleus. A lesion here typically causes parkinsonism or dystonia, not hemiballismus.
    - **SNc:** Located ventral to the STN and medial to the crus of the substantia nigra. Lesions cause Parkinsonism due to dopamine depletion.
    - **Caudate Nucleus:** Located superior and lateral to the putamen, forming the striatum. Lesions can cause various movement disorders, cognitive deficits, or psychiatric symptoms.
    - **Putamen:** Located lateral to the globus pallidus and medial to the internal capsule, forming the striatum. Lesions can cause contralateral hemiparesis or movement disorders.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right-sided hemiballismus is caused by a lesion on the contralateral (left) side. The CT scan localizes the hemorrhage to the left internal capsule. Given the anatomical location of the STN medial to the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule, this structure is the most likely site of injury causing the observed hemiballismus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi):** The GPi is located lateral to the internal capsule, within the lentiform nucleus. While it's part of the basal ganglia circuit, a lesion in the internal capsule is less likely to directly affect the GPi compared to the STN, which is immediately medial to it. Lesions of the GPi itself or its inputs typically cause parkinsonism or dystonia, not hemiballismus.
- (B) **Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc):** The SNc is located ventral to the STN and medial to the crus of the substantia nigra. While also part of the basal ganglia, it is not immediately adjacent to the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule. Lesions of the SNc primarily cause Parkinson's disease due to dopamine neuron loss.
- (D) **Caudate Nucleus:** The caudate nucleus is located superior and lateral to the putamen and internal capsule. A hemorrhage in the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule would not typically affect the caudate nucleus. Lesions here are associated with various motor, cognitive, and behavioral changes.
- (E) **Putamen:** The putamen is located lateral to the globus pallidus and medial to the internal capsule. While adjacent to the internal capsule, the STN is more specifically located medial to the retrolenticular part, making it the most vulnerable structure in this specific location for a hemorrhage causing hemiballismus. Lesions of the putamen can cause contralateral hemiparesis or movement disorders.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure              | Location Relative to Internal Capsule (Retrolenticular Part) | Primary Function/Lesion Effect                                  | Risk from Retrolenticular IC Hemorrhage |
|-----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| Subthalamic Nucleus (STN) | Medial                                                    | Indirect pathway; Disinhibition leads to hyperkinesia (hemiballismus) | High                                   |
| Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi) | Lateral                                                   | Output nucleus; Inhibition of thalamus; Lesion -> Hypokinesia/Dystonia | Low                                    |
| Substantia Nigra (SNc)   | Ventral to STN, medial to crus of SN                      | Dopamine production; Lesion -> Parkinsonism                       | Low                                    |
| Caudate Nucleus        | Superior and Lateral                                        | Striatum; Motor planning, cognition; Lesion -> Various deficits   | Very Low                               |
| Putamen                | Lateral to GPi, medial to IC                               | Striatum; Motor control; Lesion -> Hemiparesis/Movement disorder | Low                                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hemiballismus is classically associated with lesions of the contralateral subthalamic nucleus (STN) or its projections. The STN is located immediately medial to the retrolenticular part of the internal capsule, making it particularly vulnerable to vascular events (like hemorrhage or infarction) affecting this region. Remember the key anatomical relationships within the basal ganglia and internal capsule to localize lesions causing specific movement disorders. Differentiate hemiballismus (STN lesion) from Parkinsonism (SNc lesion) or dystonia/hypokinesia (GPi lesion).

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 2 Focus 50 (Putamen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structure of the basal ganglia (putamen) based on its relationship to other structures, its embryological origin, and its functional connectivity, specifically its role in motor control and its vulnerability to vascular compromise. (识别基底神经节（壳核）的解剖结构，基于其与其他结构的联系、其胚胎学起源及其功能连接，特别是其在运动控制中的作用及其对血管损伤的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset right-sided hemiparesis and facial droop. He reports that the symptoms began suddenly while he was eating breakfast. Neurological examination reveals weakness predominantly affecting the right arm and leg (Medical Research Council grade 3/5), right facial weakness (lower face more pronounced than upper face), and mild dysarthria. Sensory examination is normal. Deep tendon reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows a small, well-defined area of hypodensity in the left basal ganglia, specifically involving the lateral portion. The patient's symptoms do not improve significantly over the next 24 hours despite supportive care.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by the lesion described in the CT scan?

(A) Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi)
(B) Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc)
(C) Subthalamic Nucleus (STN)
(D) Putamen
(E) Caudate Nucleus

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This suggests a potential vascular event like a stroke.
- **Symptoms:** Acute onset right-sided hemiparesis (arm and leg weakness) and right facial droop (lower face > upper face). This pattern points to an upper motor neuron lesion affecting the corticospinal and corticobulbar tracts contralaterally to the side of the lesion. The sparing of the upper face suggests involvement of the corticobulbar tract *above* the facial nucleus (pons), consistent with a supranuclear lesion.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (MRC 3/5), right lower facial weakness, dysarthria, hyperreflexia on the right. These findings confirm an upper motor neuron lesion on the left side.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a small, well-defined hypodensity in the *left basal ganglia*, specifically the *lateral portion*. This indicates an acute ischemic infarct (stroke) in that region.
- **Question:** Identify the specific structure within the left basal ganglia, particularly the lateral portion, that is most likely affected.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Anatomy:** The basal ganglia are a group of subcortical nuclei involved in motor control, learning, executive functions, and emotions. Key components include the striatum (caudate nucleus and putamen), globus pallidus (internal and external segments), subthalamic nucleus, and substantia nigra (pars compacta and pars reticulata).
- **Putamen:** The putamen is the lateral part of the striatum. It is located lateral to the globus pallidus and medial to the internal capsule. It receives input from the cerebral cortex (especially motor, premotor, and somatosensory areas) and projects to the globus pallidus and substantia nigra.
- **Location:** The clinical vignette specifically mentions the *lateral portion* of the basal ganglia. The putamen occupies this lateral position within the basal ganglia complex. The globus pallidus is medial to the putamen. The caudate nucleus is superior and anterior to the putamen. The STN is ventral to the thalamus. The SNc is in the midbrain.
- **Vascular Supply:** The basal ganglia, particularly the putamen and globus pallidus, are supplied by branches of the middle cerebral artery (MCA), specifically the lenticulostriate arteries (also known as paramedian arteries). These small arteries are prone to occlusion, especially in patients with hypertension and atherosclerosis, leading to lacunar infarcts. The lateral portion of the basal ganglia (putamen) is a common site for such infarcts.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Damage to the putamen, often due to lacunar infarcts affecting the lenticulostriate arteries, typically results in contralateral motor deficits, including hemiparesis, hemiballismus (less common), or dystonia. The patient's presentation of acute right hemiparesis and facial droop following a stroke affecting the left basal ganglia (lateral portion) is highly consistent with a lesion involving the putamen. The sparing of the upper face in the facial droop indicates a supranuclear lesion, which is consistent with damage to the corticobulbar tract fibers passing through or near the putamen/internal capsule.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi):** The GPi is medial to the putamen. While it is part of the basal ganglia and can be affected by strokes, the vignette specifically points to the *lateral* portion, making the putamen more likely. GPi lesions are more associated with dystonia and parkinsonism.
- (B) **Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc):** The SNc is located in the midbrain, not the basal ganglia proper (though functionally related). It is primarily involved in dopamine production and is the main site of degeneration in Parkinson's disease. It is not typically affected by the type of lacunar infarct described in the vignette.
- (C) **Subthalamic Nucleus (STN):** The STN is located ventral to the thalamus, inferior to the internal capsule. Lesions here typically cause hemiballismus (violent, involuntary flinging movements of the limbs), which is not described in the patient.
- (E) **Caudate Nucleus:** The caudate nucleus is superior and anterior to the putamen, forming the anterior part of the striatum. While it can be affected by strokes, the description of the *lateral* basal ganglia points more specifically to the putamen. Caudate lesions are often associated with cognitive and behavioral changes, as well as chorea (e.g., Huntington's disease).

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location Relative to Putamen | Key Function(s)                                  | Typical Lesion Presentation                               | Embryological Origin      |
|----------------------|------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Putamen (Correct)** | Lateral part of striatum     | Motor control, procedural learning                 | Contralateral hemiparesis, hemiballismus, dystonia        | Telencephalon (Neopallium) |
| Globus Pallidus Internus (A) | Medial to Putamen          | Output nucleus of basal ganglia (inhibitory)      | Dystonia, parkinsonism                                   | Telencephalon (Pallidum)   |
| Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (B) | Midbrain                  | Dopamine production (motor control)              | Parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor)           | Mesencephalon             |
| Subthalamic Nucleus (C) | Ventral Thalamus            | Excitation of GPi/SNr (motor control)            | Hemiballismus                                             | Diencephalon              |
| Caudate Nucleus (E)    | Superior/Anterior to Putamen | Motor control, cognition, emotion, procedural learning | Chorea, cognitive/behavioral changes, dystonia           | Telencephalon (Neopallium) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The putamen is the lateral component of the striatum within the basal ganglia. It is highly susceptible to lacunar infarcts due to its blood supply from the lenticulostriate arteries branching off the MCA. Lesions here typically cause contralateral motor deficits like hemiparesis. Differentiating the putamen from the medial globus pallidus, superior caudate nucleus, ventral subthalamic nucleus, and midbrain substantia nigra based on location and clinical presentation is crucial. Remember the mnemonic "G-P-C-S" (Globus Pallidus, Putamen, Caudate, Subthalamic Nucleus) for the basal ganglia nuclei, noting their relative positions.
